===== standata-nbc2023-interpretation-23-bci-002 =====

STANDATA interpretation 23-BCI-002/23-FCI-009 
Building/Fire  
  
Overnight child care 
Date Issued:  April 2024 
Page 1 of 4 
Purpose 
To clarify applicable requirements of the National Building Code - 2023 Alberta Edition (NBC(AE)) and the 
National Fire Code - 2023 Alberta Edition (NFC(AE)) for occupancies licensed to provide overnight child care 
services. 
Discussion 
Child care services are classified as Group C residential occupancies for dayhomes or as Gr oup A, Division 2 
assembly occupancies for daycares.  Changes in the Early Learning and Child Care Act (ELCCA) regulations and 
policies in early 2021 resulted in the expansion of accommodations for overnight child care services. This 
interpretation has been developed to provide guidance on the correct application of the Safety Codes Act for 
these accommodations as regulated under the ELCCA.   
For the purposes of this interpretation, Children and Family Services refer to services provided by an organization 
that is responsible for a child for a period not exceeding 24 consecutive hours.  
 
Free copies of the NBC(AE) and the NFC(AE) are available for download at the following links:  
• National Building Code – 2023 Alberta Edition 
• National Fire Code - 2023 Alberta Edition 
Code References 
Division A, Article 1.4.1.2.  
1.4.1.2. Defined Terms 
 
Smoke alarm means a combined smoke detector and audible alarm device designed to sound an alarm within 
the room or suite in which it is located upon the detection of smoke within that room or suite. 
 
Smoke detector means a fire detector designed to operate when the concentration of airborne combustion 
products exceeds a predetermined level. 
 
Sentences 3.2.4.20.(1) and (2) 
3.2.4.20. Smoke Alarms  
1) Except as provided in Article 3.2.4.21., smoke alarms shall be installed in accordance with this Article.  
2) Except as required by Sentence (5) and permitted by Sentence (10), smoke alarms conforming to 
CAN/
---Application---
The following interpretation applies to dayhomes or daycares providing child  care services with overnight sleeping 
in accordance with the ELCCA. 
Interpretation 
1. Residential Occupancy Dayhome: 
• Smoke alarm installation in compliance with the NBC(AE) which includes but not limited to:  
- Smoke alarm in every sleeping room or area 
- Smoke alarm on each level of the residence 
- Smoke alarm between the sleeping and living area 
- Smoke alarms interconnected so that if one alarm is activated all alarms will sound 
 
• In lieu of smoke alarms, the installation of a residential fire warning system installed in conformance to 
CAN/ULC-S540. 
• Carbon monoxide alarm or alarms installation in compliance with the NBC(AE).  
• Egress window or door to the exterior from sleeping rooms or area (unless sprinklered).  
• It is recommended a minimum of two fire extinguishers, one by each exit, be maintained on site.  
• It is recommended the dayhome have a fire safety plan and conduct regular fire drills. 
• Dayhomes are classified as a Group C residential occupancy and are not required to have a fire 
inspection. Upon request or complaint, the Authority Having Jurisdiction (AHJ) may conduct and enforce 
a fire inspection. The AHJ will determine, based on their Quality Management Plan and the Safety 
Codes Act, if they are able to provide and enforce a fire inspection. 
 
2. Assembly Occupancy with total sleeping accommodations of 10 or less:  
• Awake overnight staff. 
• Smoke alarm installation in compliance with the NBC(AE) which includes but not limited to:  
- Smoke alarm in every sleeping room or area 
- Smoke alarm on each level 
- Smoke alarm between the sleeping and living area 
- Smoke alarms interconnected so that if one alarm is activated all alarms will sound  
 
• Fire alarm system in compliance to interpretation (3) is acceptable in lieu of smoke alarms.  
• Installation of carbon monoxide alarm or alarms. 
• Egress window or door to the exterior from sleeping rooms or area (unless sprinklered).  
• In lieu of egress window or door to the exterior, provide at least two separate means of egress from 
suite. 
• Requires a minimum of two fire extinguishers, one by each exit (NFPA10). 
• Fire safety plan. 
 
3. Assembly Occupancy with total sleeping accommodations of more than 10: 
• Awake overnight staff. 
• Fire alarm system. 
• Smoke detector in each

- Smoke alarm in every sleeping room or area 
- Smoke alarm on each level 
- Smoke alarm between the sleeping and living area 
- Smoke alarms interconnected so that if one alarm is activated all alarms will sound  
 
• Fire alarm system in compliance to interpretation (3) is acceptable in lieu of smoke alarms.  
• Installation of carbon monoxide alarm or alarms. 
• Egress window or door to the exterior from sleeping rooms or area (unless sprinklered).  
• In lieu of egress window or door to the exterior, provide at least two separate means of egress from 
suite. 
• Requires a minimum of two fire extinguishers, one by each exit (NFPA10).

3. Assembly Occupancy with total sleeping accommodations of more than 10:

• Fire alarm system.

• Installation of carbon monoxide alarm or alarms.

===== standata-nbc2023-interpretation-23-bci-003 =====
 Building STANDATA | Alberta.ca

STANDATA interpretation 23-BCI-003/23-FCI-002 
Building/Fire 
Occupant loads 
Date Issued:   April 2024 
Page 1 of 9 
Purpose 
To provide a consistent method for calculating occupant loads for assembly occupancies under the National 
Building Code – 2023 Alberta Edition (NBC(AE)) and N ational Fire Code – 2023 Alberta Edition (NFC(AE)).  
Discussion 
Over the years, it has been identified occupant loads may be calculated differently from one jurisdiction to 
another. This created confusion for  both the business owner and the authority having jurisdiction (AHJ) during  
enforcement.  
The intent statement for occupant loads issued for the National Building Code of Canada (NBC) clarify the 
calculation of occupant loads is to limit the probability of overcrowding, which could lead to delayed egress during 
an emergency evacuation and to determine the minimum design load to  calculate egress and exit capacity. The 
intent statement is also used to  determine the required calculations when rooms or areas are intended for 
different occupant loads at different times.  
The intent statement for occupant loads in the National Fire Code of Canada (NFC) indicate it is to limit the 
probability that a room will contain too many occupants, which could lead to overcrowding or insufficient capacity 
of the means of egress.  
This indicates the intent of occupant loads in both the NBC and NFC is to limit the probability that delays in 
evacuation or movement of persons to a safe place in an emergency which could lead to harm to a person. 
The NBC(AE) and NFC(AE) have been written to harmonize with the NBC and NFC . D esign occupant loads are 
the responsibility of and provided by the registered architectural professional or designer.  
For buildings which require a registered architectural professional or designer, incorporating the exit occupant 
load into the design of is the responsibility of the registered architectural professional in accorda
---Application---
This interpretation applies to the calculation of occupant loads in accordance with the NBC(AE) and NFC(AE). 
Inte
rpretation 
All stakeholders should be aware of the requirements used to calculate design occupant loads (based on the 
NBC(AE)) and the maximum permissible occupant loads (based on the NFC(AE) ) and engage with the 
appropriate AHJs accordingly. In any jurisdiction, a collaborative approach between all stakeholders is necessary 
to reach an occupant load capacity for any use or application.  
In all cases, the lowest number for the maximum occupant load must be used.  
The maximum occupant load of a room or floor area for an assembly occupancy, or licensed beverage 
establishment, shall be the l owest number derived by Criteria #1, #2, #3, and #4.  
1. CRITERIA #1 – FIRE ALARM
• A building is required to have a fire alarm system, as per NBC(AE), when the building is sprinklered, or contains
- a total occupant load more than 300, other than in open seating areas ,
- an occupant load more than 150 above or below the first storey, other than in open air seating areas ,
- a school, college, or child care facility, including daycare facility for children, with an occupant load more than
40, 
- a licensed beverage establishment or a licensed restaurant, in accordance with Alberta Gaming, Liquor and
Cannabis, with an occupant load more than 150, or 
- an occupant load more than 300 below an open -air seating area.
If the building is not provided with a fire alarm system, the occupant load is restricted to the total occupant load 
described above.  
2. CRITERIA #2 – BUILDING DESIGN OCCUPANT LOAD
• The 
building and the assembly occupancies must be designed to accommodate the maximum design occupant
load to ensure structural sufficiency, adequate ventilation for health, and sufficient number of washrooms are
provided.
3. CRIT
ERIA #3 – DENSITY AND NET FLOOR SPACE
• Net
 floor space is the amount of floor area when the gross floor area of a room intended for assembly  is taken
and is then deduct ed by all area occupied by structures, equipment, and furniture. Parts of the floor area used
for circulation such as an aisle serving entrances and exits, aisles to kitchens, bars and washrooms, must also
be deducted. In general, the net floor space in an assembly occupancy is where the public is expected to
assemble and, in case of emergency, be able to move to

STANDATA interpretation 23-BCI-003R1/23-FCI-002R1 
Building/Fire 
Occupant loads 
Date Issued:  January 2025 
Page 1 of 9 
Purpose 
To provide a consistent method for calculating occupant loads for assembly occupancies under the National 
Building Code – 2023 Alberta Edition (NBC(AE)) and National Fire Code – 2023 Alberta Edition (NFC(AE)). 
Discussion 
Over the years, it has been identified occupant loads may be calculated differently from one jurisdiction to 
another. This created confusion for both the business owner and the authority having jurisdiction (AHJ) during 
enforcement.  
The intent statement for occupant loads issued for the National Building Code of Canada (NBC) clarify the 
calculation of occupant loads is to limit the probability of overcrowding, which could lead to delayed egress during 
an emergency evacuation and to determine the minimum design load to calculate egress and exit capacity. The 
intent statement is also used to determine the required calculations when rooms or areas are intended for 
different occupant loads at different times.  
The intent statement for occupant loads in the National Fire Code of Canada (NFC) indicate it is to limit the 
probability that a room will contain too many occupants, which could lead to overcrowding or insufficient capacity 
of the means of egress.  
This indicates the intent of occupant loads in both the NBC and NFC is to limit the probability that delays in 
evacuation or movement of persons to a safe place in an emergency which could lead to harm to a person. 
The NBC(AE) and NFC(AE) have been written to harmonize with the NBC and NFC. Design occupant loads are 
the responsibility of and provided by the registered professional or designer.  
For buildings which require a registered architectural professional or designer, incorporating the exit occupant 
load into the design is the responsibility of the registered architectural professional in accordance with the pract
---Application---
This interpretation applies to the calculation of occupant loads in accordance with the NBC(AE) and NFC(AE). 
Interpretation 
All stakeholders should be aware of the requirements used to calculate design occupant loads (based on the 
NBC(AE)) and the maximum permissible occupant loads (based on the NFC(AE)) and engage with the 
appropriate AHJs accordingly. In any jurisdiction, a collaborative approach between all stakeholders is necessary 
to reach an occupant load capacity for any use or application. 
In all cases, the lowest number for the maximum occupant load must be used.  
The maximum occupant load of a room or floor area for an assembly occupancy, or licensed beverage 
establishment, must be the lowest number derived by Criteria #1, #2, #3, and #4. 
1. CRITERIA #1 – FIRE ALARM
• A building is required to have a fire alarm system, as per NBC(AE), when the building is sprinklered, or contains
­ a total occupant load more than 300, other than in open seating areas,
­ an occupant load more than 150 above or below the first storey, other than in open air seating areas ,
­ a school, college, or child care facility, including daycare facility for children, with an occupant load more than
40, 
­ a licensed beverage establishment or a licensed restaurant, in accordance with Alberta Gaming, Liquor and 
Cannabis Act (AGLC), with an occupant load more than 150, or  
­ an occupant load more than 300 below an open-air seating area. 
If the building is not provided with a fire alarm system, the occupant load is restricted to the total occupant load 
described above. 
Note: the NBC(AE) does not define a licensed beverage establishment or a licensed restaurant. The AGLC Act 
provides a definition for a “licensed premises” which may assist when assigning an occupant load for a licensed 
beverage establishment or a licensed restaurant. 
2. CRITERIA #2 – BUILDING DESIGN OCCUPANT LOAD
• The building and the assembly occupancies must be designed to accommodate the maximum design occupant
load to ensure structural sufficiency, adequate ventilation for health, and sufficient number of washrooms are
provided.
3. CRITERIA #3 – NET FLOOR SPACE
• Net floor space is the amount of floor area when the gross floor area of a room intended for assembly  is taken
and is then deducted by all area occupied by structures, equipment, and furniture. Parts of the floor area used
by the occu

===== standata-nbc2023-interpretation-23-bci-004 =====
Building STANDATA | Alberta.ca  Fire STANDATA | Alberta.ca  
Electrical STANDATA | Alberta.ca Gas STANDATA | Alberta.ca  
 
©2024 Government of Alberta  |  Published: Apri l 2024 |  Municipal Affairs  
 
STANDATA interpretation 23-BCI-004/23-FCI-015/22-LEG-
PR-002/20-GCB-017 
Building/Fire/Electrical/Gas  
  
NFPA 33 spray booths, application of the safety codes 
Date Issued:   April 2024 
Page 1 of 3  
Purpose 
This clarifies the requirements for permitting under the Safety Codes Act  (SCA) and Permit Regulation for the  
installation of spray booths, spray areas, and spray rooms.  
Discussion 
There have been inconsistencies regarding the application of the National Building Code -  2023 Alberta Edition 
(NBC(AE)) and National Fire Code - 2023 Alberta Edition ( NFC(AE)) related to the N ational Fire Protection 
Association  (NFPA) 33, “Standard for Spray Application Using Flammable or Combust ible Materials,” and the 
requirement for applicable safety codes permits under the Permit Regulation pursuant to the SCA . 
The NBC(AE) and the NFC(AE) are companion documents with many cross references. The authority having 
jurisdiction (AHJ) would apply th e SCA and the Permit Regulation to installations which fall within the scope of 
NFPA 33.  For example, the following would require safety codes permits:  
1. HVAC system,  
2. fire suppression system,  
3. electrical system , and  
4. gas system.  
 
The installation of a spray booth system would be under the direction of a registered professional for design to be 
conducted under good engineering practice. The NBC(AE) further directs the user to the NFC(AE)  by reference in 
Article 6.9.1.2. of the NBC(AE) .  The NFC(AE) specifically references the NFPA 33 standard.   Additionally,  NFPA 
33 is identified in the appendix  of the NBC(AE)  as an NFPA standard for use by the registered professional.  
 
Permit Regulation pursuant to the Safety Codes Act 
Building permit 
6(1) A permit in the building discipline is required for the following:  
(a) the construction of a building, including the renovation of  or addition
---Application---
This joint interpretation applies to systems serving spaces that contain hazardous gases, dusts , or liquids (such 
as spray booths) where specific design, construction, and/or installation requirements are not stated in the 
NBC(AE) and good engineering practice is required to be utilized.  
Interpretation 
1. The publication of the NFPA  standard applicable  to systems serving spaces for spray application of 
flammable of combustible material is NFPA 33, “Standard for Spray Application Using Flammable or 
Combustible Materials.”  
2. Applicable safety codes permits are required for the building, electrical , and gas disciplines.  
 
This INTERPRETATION is applicable throughout the Province of Alberta.  
Unless stated otherwise, all Code references in this STANDATA to the National Building Code -  2023 Alberta 
Edition and the National Fire Code -  2023 Alberta Edition

2. Applicable safety codes permits are required for the building, electrical , and gas disciplines.  
 
This INTERPRETATION is applicable throughout the Province of Alberta.

===== standata-nbc2023-interpretation-23-bci-008 =====
 
 
Building STANDATA | Alberta.ca  
©2024 Government of Alberta  |  October 2024  |  Municipal Affairs 
 
     
STANDATA interpretation 23-BCI-008 
Building  
  
Smoke detectors in smoke or fire/smoke damper installation 
Date Issued:  October 2024 
Page 1 of 4 
Purpose 
Clarifies whether the National Building Code – 2023 Alberta Edition (NBC(AE)) requires a smoke detector as part 
of a smoke or fire/smoke damper operation to be connected to the fire alarm system (FAS).  
Discussion 
In accordance with Sentence 3.1.8.11.(3) smoke damper and combination smoke/fire dampers must be 
configured to close automatically upon a signal from an adjacent smoke detector.  
The requirements specified in Sentence 3.1.8.11.(3) pertain exclusively to the location of the smoke detector. This 
provision does not require adhering to any additional fire alarm system requirements. The smoke detector 
mentioned here is meant to operate as part of the damper system and is not intended to connect to or require the 
need for a FAS.  
As per NBC(AE) Sentence 3.1.8.5.(3) smoke dampers must conform to NFPA 105, Article 6.3.2.4. 
Where smoke detectors are used to control smoke dampers or fire/smoke dampers the detectors are required to 
comply with NFPA 72.  
The confusion may stem from the definition of a smoke detector in the NBC(AE) where it is defined as a type of 
fire detector. A fire detector is defined as a device that sends a signal, to the fire alarm system.  
Although the NBC(AE) provides a definition for a fire detector, there are no mandated requirements for a smoke 
detector serving a smoke damper or combination smoke/fire damper to send a signal to the FAS.  
 
Code References  
Division A, Sentence 1.4.1.2.(1) 
 
Smoke detector means a fire detector designed to operate when the concentration of airborne 
combustion products exceeds a predetermined level. 
 
Fire detector means a device that detects a fire condition and automatically initiates an electrical signal 
to actuate an alert signal or alarm signal and includes heat detectors and smoke detectors. 
 
 
 
 
 
STANDATA 2
---Application---
This interpretation applies to smoke detectors used for initiating the operation of smoke dampers or fire/smoke 
dampers in HVAC systems for buildings constructed in conformance with the NBC(AE). 
Interpretation 
1. Smoke dampers or fire/smoke dampers are intended to control the spread of smoke or fire/smoke in 
specified locations. 
 
2. Smoke detectors used to initiate the operation of smoke dampers or fire/smoke dampers are permitted to 
be either supervisory or alarm initiating devices. 
 
3. Smoke detectors located as described in CAN/ULC-S524 and only intended to activate smoke dampers 
and combination smoke/fire dampers are not required to be connected to a fire alarm system if the 
system is not required in the building by Article 3.2.4.1. Sentence 3.1.8.11.(3) does not extend the 
requirement for a fire alarm system beyond the requirement in Article 3.2.4.1. 
 
4. Smoke detectors used as an initiating device for smoke or fire/smoke dampers  are not required to be 
connected to a building FAS. 
 
This INTERPRETATION is applicable throughout the Province of Alberta.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
---Interpretation---
1. Smoke dampers or fire/smoke dampers are intended to control the spread of smoke or fire/smoke in 
specified locations. 
 
2. Smoke detectors used to initiate the operation of smoke dampers or fire/smoke dampers are permitted to 
be either supervisory or alarm initiating devices. 
 
3. Smoke detectors located as described in CAN/ULC-S524 and only intended to activate smoke dampers 
and combination smoke/fire dampers are not required to be connected to a fire alarm system if the 
system is not required in the building by Article 3.2.4.1. Sentence 3.1.8.11.(3) does not extend the 
requirement for a fire alarm system beyond the requirement in Article 3.2.4.1. 
 
4. Smoke detectors used as an initiating device for smoke or fire/smoke dampers  are not required to be 
connected to a building FAS.

STANDATA interpretation 23-FCI-004/23-BCI-009 
Fire/Building  
  
Fixed fire suppression and exhaust systems – cooking, cleaning, maintenance and 
qualified persons 
Date Issued:  December  2024 
Page 1 of 15 
Purpose 
 
This clarifies the requirements from the National Building Code – 2023 Alberta Edition (NBC(AE)) and National 
Fire Code – 2023 Alberta Edition (NFC(AE)) for fixed fire suppression and exhaust systems for commercial 
cooking.  
 
Discussion 
Owners, designers, and safety code officers (SCO) have asked Municipal Affairs for guidance regarding fixed fire 
suppression and exhaust systems of non-residential cooking installations and the cleaning thereof. The following 
responses are intended to address the most raised questions in applying both the NBC(AE) and NFC(AE) for 
fixed fire suppression and exhaust systems for cooking as well as the requirements for cleaning. 
 
Application 
There are standards that apply to fixed fire suppression and exhaust systems in commercial kitchens.  
  
The installation of fixed fire suppression and exhaust systems is under the jurisdiction of the NBC(AE). The 
authority having jurisdiction (AHJ) in the building discipline must be consulted for building permit and professional 
involvement requirements.  
 
The use, inspection, testing, cleaning and maintenance of fixed fire suppression and exhaust systems are 
under the jurisdiction of the NFC(AE). As such, the AHJ in the fire discipline should be consulted with regards to 
the use, inspection, testing and maintenance of these systems.  
 
The NBC(AE) and the NFC(AE) sets out the minimum requirements for fixed fire suppression and exhaust 
systems. Through these two codes, standards in the National Fire Protection Association (NFPA) documents are 
referenced, including: 
1. NFPA 96  “Standard for Ventilation Control and Fire Protection of Commercial Cooking Operations ”  
2. NFPA 10  “Standard for Portable Fire Extinguishers”

be produced in a normal household environment, then it would be appropriate to apply the requirements

===== standata-nbc2023-interpretation-23-bci-010 =====

STANDATA interpretation 23-BCI-010/23-FCI-013 
Building/Fire  
  
Special events 
Date Issued:  January 2025 
Page 1 of 15 
Purpose 
This identifies the Safety Code requirements and enforcement for indoor and outdoor special events.  
Discussion 
Safety Codes Officers (SCO), owners, facilitators, coordinators, promoters, vendors and other Ministries have 
asked Municipal Affairs for guidance on the requirements and enforcement of permits and occupant loads for 
indoor and outdoor special events.  
Some municipalities have a special event (indoor or outdoor) process that would see the applicant engage with 
multiple levels of both municipal and government departments. Part of the engagement may be with building 
and/or fire safety codes officers for the issuance of permit(s), an occupant load and approval of emergency 
plan(s) or a site plan(s). The process and processing of special events, both indoor and outdoor special events, 
are at the discretion of the Municipality in which the event is occurring. Individuals planning a special event should 
inquire with the Municipality on requirements and the process involved.  
The Safety Codes Act, the National Building Code – 2023 Alberta Edition (NBC(AE)) and National Fire Code – 
2023 Alberta Edition (NFC(AE)) have provisions that provide guidance on the issuance and enforcement of 
occupant load(s) and emergency plan(s) if one is required.  
This document provides guidance on the minimum safety codes standards and requirements necessary to 
establish an acceptable level of life safety during any type of special event.   
It is important to note there are other regulatory bodies with minimum standards which must be met. This 
STANDATA does not apply to those other regulatory bodies and their levels of enforcement however, the 
assumption is all parties involved with the special event will work together to achieve compliance with all 
municipal bylaws and provincial regulations.  
Co
---Application---
This interpretation applies to indoor and outdoor special events requiring a permit, occupant load(s) and/or 
emergency planning requires the involvement and approval of the local authority having jurisdiction (AHJ).  
 
Interpretation 
 
The issuance of permits and enforcement of safety codes, including the enforcement of building, electrical, fire, 
plumbing and gas codes, is identified through the Safety Codes Act and its regulations. These codes apply to 
both indoor and outdoor special events and their associated activities.   
How a Municipality decides to conduct the inspection of any special event, indoor or outdoor event or activity is 
their decision. Most municipalities work in conjunction with other parties (i.e., Alberta Health Services, other SCOs 
from various disciplines, AGLC, Municipal Business Licensing, RCMP or Municipal Police and Bylaw Services, 
etc.) to create an inspection process and/or a permitting process to assist all parties involved with completing 
these event inspections. Other municipalities conduct these inspections independently and there is no 
requirement to obtain a special event permit. Regardless of the process used to allow any special event, indoor or 
outdoor activity or event, the Alberta safety codes is applied to these venues.  
Emergency Planning  
The emergency plan created for these events involves various entities for the safe evacuation of occupants for 
any type of event. Emergency plans are typically reviewed by a Fire SCO, and are written to assist the persons 
hosting the event with the safe evacuation of patrons and for the local emergency personal to be aware of type of 
event, hazards associated with the event, evacuation procedures, location of medical tents, muster points and 
occupant capacity, etc.  
STANDATA 23-BCI-010/23-FCI-013  Page 13 of 14 
Building STANDATA | Alberta.ca 
Fire STANDATA | Alberta.ca 
©2025 Government of Alberta  |  January 2025  | Municipal Affairs 
 
  
Emergency planning may include crowd control management based on the size and location of the event. This 
would be at the discretion of the AHJ and is usually in support of other agencies. Crowd control management may 
require the review and input from other agencies such as the local police service, bylaw, health services, 
occupational health, and safety, etc.  
It is not uncommon for an AHJ to request verification of the

===== standata-nbc2023-interpretation-23-bci-011 =====
 
Building STANDATA | Alberta.ca 
Fire STANDATA | Alberta.ca 
Electrical STANDATA | Alberta.ca 
 
©2025 Government of Alberta  |  March 2025  | Municipal Affairs 
  
     
STANDATA interpretation 23-BCI-011/24-ECI-009/23-FCI-
006 
Building/Electrical/Fire  
  
Existing fire alarm systems 
Date Issued:   March 2025 
Page 1 of 12 
Purpose 
To clarify requirements where an existing fire alarm system (FAS), regardless of age, requires inspection, testing, 
maintenance, and installation of devices or other equipment (e.g., maglocks) to  provide or maintain an expected 
level of safety for occupants.   
In addition, this interpretation defines who can install, verify, inspect, test, and maintain an FAS. 
Discussion 
There are five types of activities conducted on an FAS: installation, verification, inspection, testing, and 
maintenance.  
 
1. Installation includes a new install or additions, modifications, and renovations (changes) to an existing 
installation. 
 
2. Verification involves inspection and testing for verifying the FAS conforms to the design, and applicable codes 
and standards, and performs all its intended functions. 
 
3. Inspection is a visual examination to determine the device or system will apparently perform in accordance 
with its intended function. 
 
4. Testing is the operation of a device or system to determine it will perform in accordance with its intended 
operation or function. 
 
5. Maintenance is the removal, replacement, or servicing of devices or equipment found inoperative during an 
inspection and testing of the FAS. This would include the discovery of an inoperative device at any other time 
outside of the scheduled mandatory inspections.   
 
The assessment of the existing FAS design may impact the extent of work required to maintain safety standards. 
This includes systems that were installed following "good engineering practices" prior to the adoption of current 
codes and standards. 
 
It also highlights the importance of considering the FAS installed in accordance with established codes and 
standards when planning maintenance.
---Application---
This interpretation applies to all buildings where an FAS is currently in existence and the fire alarm undergoes 
installation, verification, inspection, testing, or maintenance.  
Interpretation 
1. Only those qualified as per the NFC(AE) can complete these types of activities.  
 
2. Qualified electricians and fire alarm technicians can complete maintenance activities as per NFC(AE) Division 
C, but only qualified electricians can replace or repair wiring.  
 
3. For an FAS installed under previous Alberta editions of the building code, the replacement of devices with 
equivalent devices is permitted (like-for-like device replacement). However, if the replacement is not an equal 
exchange, the following scenarios are to be followed: 
 
• Change of use or modernization of the building: the current NBC(AE) applies. 
• No change of use: in this situation, upgrading to the current NBC(AE) could be onerous and according to the 
NFC(AE) Division A, Article 1.1.1.1., the owner may apply to the authority having jurisdiction (AHJ) (of the 
NBC(AE)) for an alternative solution detailing the reasons why they need a fire alarm replaced and why they 
want to install a new fire alarm to an older edition of the Alberta Building Code and/or CAN/ULC standard.  
NOTE: Where the NBC(AE) requires professional involvement for a building due to its size, occupancy, or 
complexity, a registered engineering professional is also required to witness verification of upgrades or 
modifications on an existing FAS.  
If an FAS requires additions or modifications, these changes are still categorized as installs and, along with 
completely new installs, can only be completed by a qualified electrician.  
 
The following chart shows the work which an electrician or fire alarm technician can perform:

STANDATA interpretation 23-BCI-013 
Building  
Approved organization - ICC Evaluation Service 
Date Issued:   February 2025 
Page 1 of 2  
Purpose  
To recognize an organization which issues evaluation reports used in determining compliance with the National 
Building Code –  2023 Alberta Edition (NBC(AE))  as being an appro ved organization in Alberta.  
Discussion  
The Canadian Construction Materials Centre (CCMC) under the National Research Council of Canada is already 
recognized within the NBC(AE) as an approved organization which issues evaluation reports which may be used 
in determining compliance with the NBC(AE).  
There are other third-party accredited organizations which also issue evaluation reports for the same purpose.  
These organizations will require the approval of the Provincial Building Administrator for their evaluation reports to 
be used in Alberta in accordance with Sentence 1.2.2.1.(2) of Division A of the NBC(AE).  
 
These organizations are accredited by the Standards Council of Canada (SCC) for certifying products where 
there are applicable standards.  
 
Code References  
Division A, Sentences 1.2.2.1.(1)&(2) 
1.2.2. Materials, Appliances, Systems and Equipment  
1.2.2.1. Characteristics of Materials, Appliances, Systems and Equipment 
 
1) All materials, appliances, systems and equipment installed to meet the requirements of this Code shall 
possess the necessary characteristics to perform their  intended functions when installed in a building.  
 
2) Evaluation reports issued by the Canadian Construction Materials Centre, National Research Council of 
Canada, or an organization approved by the Provincial Building Administrator may be used in determining 
compliance with the requirements  of this Code.  
 
 
Application  
This interpretation applies to an organization that issue evaluation reports, for use in Alberta as provided for under 
the NBC(AE). 
STANDATA 23-BCI-013  Page 2 of 2 
 
 
Building codes and stan
---Application---
This interpretation applies to an organization that issue evaluation reports, for use in Alberta as provided for under 
the NBC(AE).

Building codes and standards | Alberta.ca 
©2025 Government of Alberta  |  February  2025  |  Municipal Affairs  
 
Unless stated otherwise, all Code references in this STANDATA are to the National Building Code – 2023 Alberta 
Edition  
Issued by the Provincial Building Administrator  
 
 
 
[Original signed] 
Paul Chang 
Alberta Municipal Affairs –  Technical and Corporate Services  
Email: safety.services@gov.ab.ca  Phone: 1- 866-421-6929   
To sign up for our List Subscription Service go to: municipalaffairs.gov.ab.ca/am_list_subscription_services  
Interpretation  
This interpretation recognizes ICC Evaluation Service as an approved organization that can issue evaluation 
reports which may be used in determining  compliance with the requirement s of the NBC(AE) . 
Conditions:  
1. The organization must maintain their accreditation with the SCC; and  
2. Where there is a change or lapse of their accreditation, the organization must notify the Provincial Building 
Administrator when such changes take effect.  
 
This INTERPRETATION is applicable throughout the Province of Alberta.

STANDATA 23-BCI-014  Page 1 of 4 
Building STANDATA | Alberta.ca

STANDATA interpretation 23-BCI-014 
Building  
   
Integrated testing coordinator 
Date Issued:  March 2025 
Page 1 of 4 
Purpose 
To clarify the expectation for qualifications of an “integrated testing coordinator.” 
Discussion 
Where fire protection and life safety systems and systems with fire protection and life safety functions are 
integrated with each other, the National Building Code – 2023 Alberta Edition (NBC(AE)) prescribes the systems 
to be tested in accordance with CAN/ULC-S1001, “Standard for Integrated Systems Testing of Fire Protection and 
Life Safety Systems.”  
 
CAN/ULC-S1001 requires the involvement of an “integrated testing coordinator,” and that  the integrated testing 
coordinator be knowledgeable and experienced in the design, installation, and operation of fire protection and life 
safety system(s), and the fire protection and life safety functions of building systems.  
The NBC(AE) also indicates that a “registered engineering professional” is required for certain aspects of design, 
which includes, but is not limited to, the following:  
• fire alarm systems;  
• projects where the size and complexity may give rise to special safety concerns; 
• fire protection systems; 
• mechanical life safety systems; and 
• electrical life safety systems. 
  
STANDATA 23-BCI-014  Page 2 of 4 
Building STANDATA | Alberta.ca  
©2025 Government of Alberta  |  March 2025  |  Municipal Affairs 
 
Code References  
Division A, Article 1.4.1.2.   
1.4.1.2. Defined Terms 
Registered engineering professional means an individual who is authorized to engage in the practice of 
engineering under the Engineering and Geoscience Professions Act and its Regulations.  
Registered professional means an individual who qualifies as a 
(a) registered architectural professional, 
(b) registered engineering professional, or 
(c) licensed interior designer. 
 
Division C, Sentence 2.4.1.1.(4) 
2.4.1.1. General 
4) If the size or complex
---Application---
This interpretation applies to individuals or corporations presenting themselves in the position of an “integrated 
testing coordinator” to perform the duties required in accordance with CAN/ULC-S1001, “Standard for Integrated 
Systems Testing of Fire Protection and Life Safety Systems.” 
Interpretation 
An integrated testing coordinator is considered to be a registered engineering professional who is knowledgeable 
and experienced in the design, installation, and operation of fire protection and life safety system(s), and the fire 
protection and life safety functions of building systems.  
 
 
 
This INTERPRETATION is applicable throughout the Province of Alberta.

2023 Alberta Edition  
Issued by the Provincial Building Administrator

===== standata-nbc2023-interpretation-23-bci-015 =====
Bui
lding STANDATA | Alberta.ca  
©2025 Government of Alberta  |  February  2025  |  Municipal Affairs  
STANDATA interpretation 23-BCI-015 
Building
Wood roof truss systems in Part 9 buildings 
Date Issued:   February 2025 
Page 1 of 3 
Pur
pose 
To provide clarity of the requirement for professional involvement in wood roof truss system designs used in Part 
9 buildings . 
Di
scussion  
Wood roof truss systems have become the preferred construction  method for roofs of residential and small 
commercial buildings falling under the scope of Part 9 of the National Building Code – 2023 Alberta Edition 
(NBC(AE)). These systems are customizable and can accommodate many exterior wall configurations. D ue to 
their customizability and complexity designers with specific knowledge are required to des ign the wood roof 
trusses. 
Ar
ticle 9.23.14.11. of the NBC(AE) refers to “good engineering practice” for the design of wood roof trusses and 
refers to the requirements in Subsection 4.3.1.  for the design of joint connections used in any roof truss , 
respectively. The term “good engineering practice” requires the involvement of an individual who is authorized to 
engage in the practice of engineering under the Engineering and Geoscience Professions Act and its Regulations. 
Designs carried out in accordance with Part 4 of the NBC(AE) require the designer be a registered professional 
skilled in the work concerned .  
Bas
ed on the requirements of Article 9.23.14.11., a wood roof truss system must be designed by a registered 
engineering professional, including the way trusses are connected to other trusses.  In addition to the design 
inputs for and details of individual trusses within the system, sufficient detail of how trusses are interconnected is 
the responsibility of the registered engineering professional to design and authenticate. Authentication does not 
have to occur on the roof truss layout plan provided the information of how trusses are interconnected is provided 
in a separate authenticated document. It is up to the registered engineering professional as 

===== standata-nbc2023-interpretation-23-bci-015r1 =====
 
 
Building STANDATA | Alberta.ca  
©2025 Government of Alberta  |  May 2025  |  Municipal Affairs 
 
  
STANDATA interpretation 23-BCI-015R1 
Building  
  
Design of wood trusses for roof assemblies in Part 9 buildings 
Date Issued:  May 2025 
Page 1 of 3 
 
Purpose 
To clarify the requirement for professional involvement in the design of wood trusses for roof assemblies used in 
Part 9 buildings. 
 
Discussion 
Wood roof trusses have become the preferred construction method for roof assemblies of residential and small 
commercial buildings falling under the scope of Part 9 of the National Building Code – 2023 Alberta Edition 
(NBC(AE)). These assemblies are customizable and can accommodate many exterior wall configurations. Due to 
their customizability and complexity, designers with specific knowledge are required to design wood roof trusses. 
 
Article 9.23.14.11. of the NBC(AE) refers to “good engineering practice” described in TPIC 2019, “Truss Design 
Procedures and Specifications for Light Metal Plate Connected Wood Trusses” (TPIC) for the design of wood roof 
trusses and refers to the requirements in Subsection 4.3.1. for the design of joint connections used in any roof 
truss, respectively. The term “good engineering practice” requires the involvement of an individual who is 
authorized to engage in the practice of engineering under the Engineering and Geoscience Professions Act and 
its Regulations. Designs carried out in accordance with Part 4 of the NBC(AE) require t he designer be a 
registered professional skilled in the work concerned.  
 
Based on the requirements of Article 9.23.14.11., a truss assembly constructed using wood trusses must be 
designed by a registered engineering professional, including, where applicable, the way trusses are 
interconnected. In addition to the design inputs for and details of individual truss profiles, the parameters and 
limitations for trusses which are interconnected is the responsibility of the registered engineering professional to 
design and authenticate. Authentication does not have to occur on the roof t
---Application---
This interpretation applies to the design of wood trusses for roof assemblies in Part 9 buildings that do not require 
professional involvement, as per Section 2.4. of Division C. 
 
Interpretation 
1. Wood trusses and truss assemblies must be designed and authenticated by a registered engineering 
professional. 
 
2. All aspects of wood trusses and truss assemblies required to be designed in accordance with good engineering 
practice such as that described in TPIC, require to be authenticated. Authenticated documents may include, but 
not limited to, the information provided in Appendix H of the TPIC. Documents may not require authentication 
where the design information contained within is located on other documentation that is already authenticated.  
3. All wood truss joint connections must be designed in conformance with the requirements in Subsection 4.3.1 
and be authenticated. 
 
4. The authenticated truss drawings or other required authenticated drawings must be provided to the AHJ prior to 
their scheduled inspection or approval for covering as part of the permit conditions.   
 
 
This INTERPRETATION is applicable throughout the Province of Alberta.

Barrier-free design requirements: relaxations 
Date Issued:  March 2025 
Page 1 of 3 
Purpose 
To clarify when a request for relaxation of barrier-free requirements is appropriate, and provide exempted building 
uses where a relaxation request is not required.  
Discussion 
The Provincial Barrier-Free Administrator and Provincial Building Administrator are receiving barrier-free relaxation 
requests that are either not appropriate for relaxation or lack the required review by the authority having jurisdiction 
(AHJ), i.e. accredited municipality or agency, during the building permit application stage.  
The Provincial Building Administrator and the Provincial Barrier-Free Administrator have the authority to grant or 
refuse a relaxation, in accordance with Article 2.2.1.4. of Division C of the National Building Code – 2023 Alberta 
Edition (NBC(AE)). Barrier-free relaxations may be issued where compliance with barrier-free design requirements 
are unnecessary or extraordinary circumstances prevent conformance with the requirements. These two situations 
may be appropriate for a relaxation provided the applicant has demonstrated to the satisfaction of the Provincial 
Building Administrator or Provincial Barrier-Free Administrator that valid conditions exist to support a relaxation of 
barrier-free requirements.  
Applications for barrier-free relaxations will not be accepted by Municipal Affairs for new building construction 
required to comply with barrier-free design requirements under the NBC(AE). 
New construction refers to the creation of a completely new building that has not been previously occupied or 
used. It also applies to spaces that are being created (i.e., CRUs) or utilized for the first time (no prior tenants) 
within the “building envelope.” 
Applications for barrier-free relaxations containing incomplete plans/drawings will not be reviewed by the Provincial 
Building Administrator or Provincial Barrier-F
---Application---

described under Sentence 3.8.2.1.(1).

Barrier-free design requirements: relaxations 
Date Issued:  November 2025 
Page 1 of 3 
Purpose  
To clarify when a request for relaxation of barrier-free requirements is appropriate.  
Discussion 
The Provincial Barrier-Free Administrator and Provincial Building Administrator are receiving barrier-free relaxation 
requests that are either not appropriate for relaxation or lack the required review by the authority having jurisdiction 
(AHJ) during the building permit application stage.  
Barrier-free design requirements apply to buildings as specified in Article 3.8.1.1. of the National Building Code - 
2023 Alberta Edition (NBC(AE)). All new construction, including additions and change of use as set out in Article 
3.8.1.1., are required to comply with barrier-free design requirements.  
In accordance with the NBC(AE), barrier-free relaxations may be issued where compliance with barrier-free design 
requirements are unnecessary, or extraordinary circumstances prevent conformance with the requirements. These 
situations may be appropriate for a relaxation provided the applicant has demonstrated to the satisfaction of the 
Provincial Barrier-Free Administrator that valid conditions exist to support a relaxation of barrier-free requirements.  
Applications for barrier-free relaxations will not be accepted by Municipal Affairs for new building construction 
required to comply with barrier-free design requirements under the NBC(AE). New construction refers to the 
creation of a completely new building that has not been previously occupied or used such as spaces that are being 
created (i.e., CRUs); or utilized for the first time (no prior tenants) within the “building envelope” and additions. 
Residential housing projects may create numerous adaptable or accessible dwelling units; however, land 
constraints may prevent the provision of one (1) accessible parking stall for each adaptable or accessi
---Application---

Sentence 3.8.2.1.(1) of the NBC(AE) allows the AHJ or SCO the discretion to assess building projects as exempt 
or partially exempt from barrier-free design requirements, provided the building project meets the conditions 
described under Sentence 3.8.2.1.(1).

addition to those currently described in the NBC(AE):  
• f ire and emergency medical services (EMS) dormitories, including washrooms and showers.

• abattoirs.

• l imited-use, limited-access washroom facilities, such as in transit turnarounds; and 
• t emporary structures where barrier-free design requirements are demonstrated to be unnecessary to the AHJ 
such as modulars designated as classrooms or residential sales centres.

===== standata-nbc2023-interpretation-23-bci-017 =====
Building STANDATA | Alberta.ca  
©2025 Government of Alberta  |  March 2025  | Municipal Affairs 
 
  
STANDATA interpretation 23-BCI-017 
Building  
   
Photoluminescent exit signs 
Date Issued:  March 2025 
Page 1 of 3 
Purpose 
This interpretation provides clarification regarding the required illumination of photoluminescent (PL) exit signs 
according to Articles 3.4.5.1. and 9.9.11.3. of the National Building Code – 2023 Alberta Edition (NBC(AE)). 
Discussion 
The NBC(AE) permits the use of listed PL exit signs within the scope of both Part 3 and Part 9 under Clause 
3.4.5.1.(3)(b) and Sentence 3.4.5.1.(4), and Clause 9.9.11.3.(3)(b) and Sentence 9.9.11.3.(4), respectively.  
Objectives and Functional Statements are consistent throughout Articles 3.4.5.1. and 9.9.11.3.  
The intent of Sentences 3.4.5.1.(5) and 9.9.11.3.(5) is to limit the probability that electrical circuits for the 
illumination of exit signs will not operate properly (e.g., due to normal power failure, or will fail or be disconnected 
due to deficiencies with other non-emergency electrical equipment). Operational failure of the exit signs may 
result in:   
1. the exit signs not being illuminated in an emergency; 
2. exit locations not being readily identified; 
3. delays in the evacuation or movement of persons to a safe place; and 
4. harm to persons. 
CAN/ULC-S572, “Photoluminescent and Self-Luminous Signs and Path Marking Systems,” is the standard which 
covers requirements for PL exit signs. 
Under normal power failure conditions, a PL exit sign is no longer externally illuminated but conforms to Clause 
3.4.5.1.(3)(b) as the charged sign is not powered by an electrical circuit for illumination.  
The charging light source for a photoluminescent exit sign is not to be connected to automatic timers. Under 
normal building power, continuous illumination of the exit sign is needed for compliance with Sentence 3.4.5.1.(4).  
Lighting for exit signs is exempt from certain requirements of the National Energy Code of Canada for Buildings 
2020 (NECB). 
Code References 
Sentences 3.4.5.1.(3) to
---Application---
The following interpretation applies to PL exit signs. 
Interpretation 
The charging light source provided for PL exit signs do not require connection to the emergency power supply as 
per Sentence 3.4.5.1.(5) or 9.9.11.3.(5), provided the following conditions are met: 
1. Under normal building power conditions, the PL exit signs are to be continuously illuminated as per Sentence 
3.4.5.1.(4) or 9.9.11.3.(4) and as required for charging the photoluminescent properties of the PL exit signs  so 
they will perform their function under a power failure situation.  
2. The charging lights are on a separate circuit used only for the charging lights. 
3. The control for the charging light is only accessible to authorized personnel to prevent interference with the 
equipment.  
4. The charging light type and lux conforms to the listing as required for the PL exit signs.  
5. PL exit signs are installed in accordance with the manufacturer’s listed installation instructions.  
6. The PL exit signs are labelled in accordance with the time duration for which it has been tested and listed.  
 
This INTERPRETATION is applicable throughout the Province of Alberta.

Building STANDATA | Alberta.ca

STANDATA interpretation 23-BCI-018 
Building  
Guard requirements for glazing in a window  
Date Issued:  January 2026 
Page 1 of 4 
Purpose 
To clarify the application of guard requirements when glazing in a window is positioned below the minimum height 
requirements within the National Building Code – 2023 Alberta Edition (NBC(AE)). 
Discussion 
Guards are protective barriers that prevent accidental falls where a fall hazard exists in a building. These 
hazards may include open sides of stairs, landings, balconies, mezzanines, galleries, raised walkways, or fixed 
and operable windows. When there is an elevation difference of 600 mm or more between two floors, or 
between a floor and the next lower surface, the NBC(AE) generally requires a guard to reduce the risk of injury.   
Designers may choose to use glazing in guards. It is important to acknowledge when glazing is used in a 
guard, it introduces two hazards associated with both falling and human impact. The NBC(AE) addresses 
these hazards separately. 
The NBC(AE) requires a guard where there is no wall and there is an elevation difference of 600 mm or more 
between a floor and the next lower surface. When a window is located within a wall, the window is considered 
part of that wall assembly. If a window is installed in a wall where there is an elevation difference between 
surfaces of 600mm or more, and the bottom sill is below the minimum height prescribed in the NBC(AE), the 
window must either be designed as a guard or be protected with a guard.  
If a person impacts or collides with a fixed or operable window located below the minimum height prescribed in 
the NBC(AE), and the glazing is not properly reinforced, the glass may shatter and cause injury. To limit this, 
the NBC(AE) requires the glazing to be safety glazing conforming to CAN/CGSB-12.1, “Safety Glazing,” or 
wired glass conforming to CAN/CGSB-12.11-M, “Wired Safety Glass.” In addition, if the window is openable, 
the NBC(AE) has pro
---Application---
This interpretation applies to windows located in buildings where glazing is positioned below the minimum height 
requirements in the NBC(AE) and may pose a risk of injury to persons. 
Interpretation 
1. Glazing panels that are fixed and positioned below the minimum height requirements of the NBC(AE) must:  
• a ddress the risk of injury due to fall hazards associated with glazing by: 
­ incorporating safety glazing of laminated or tempered type conforming to CAN/CGSB -12.1, “Safety 
Glazing,” or 
­ incorporating wired glass conforming to CAN/CGSB-12.11-M, “Wired Safety Glass,” and 
• address the risk of injury due to the collision hazards associated with glazing by: 
­ incorporating safety glazing of laminated or tempered type conforming to CAN/CGSB -12.1, “Safety 
Glazing,” or 
­ incorporating wired glass conforming to CAN/CGSB-12.11-M, “Wired Safety Glass.” 
 
2. Glazing panels that are part of openable windows positioned below the minimum height requirements of the 
NBC(AE) must: 
• address the risk of injury due to fall hazards associated with glazing by: 
­ incorporating a mechanism that can only be released with the use of tools or special knowledge to control 
the free swinging or sliding operation of the openable part of the window to limit any clear unobstructed 
opening to not more than 100 mm measured either vertically or horizontally, and  
­ incorporating safety glazing of laminated or tempered type conforming to CAN/CGSB -12.1, “Safety 
Glazing,” or 
­ incorporating wired glass conforming to CAN/CGSB-12.11-M, “Wired Safety Glass,” and 
• address the risk of injury due to the collision hazards associated with glazing by:  
­ incorporating safety glazing of laminated or tempered type conforming to CAN/CGSB -12.1, “Safety 
Glazing,” or 
­ incorporating wired glass conforming to CAN/CGSB-12.11-M, “Wired Safety Glass.” 
 
This INTERPRETATION is applicable throughout the Province of Alberta.  
 
 
 
 
[original signed]

Building STANDATA | Alberta.ca  
©2025 Government of Alberta  |  December 2025  |  Municipal Affairs 
 
  
STANDATA interpretation 23-BCI-019  
Building  
 
Occupancy classification for supportive living accommodations 
Date Issued:  December 2025 
Page 1 of 4 
Purpose 
To clarify the application of the Safety Codes Act (Act) and the National Building Code - 2023 Alberta Edition 
(NBC(AE)) in determining the occupancy classification of buildings intended for clients receiving various levels of 
supportive living accommodation. 
Discussion 
Designation and occupancy classification to facilities providing supportive living accommodations.  
Care occupancy 
In proposed Group B, Division 3 care occupancy buildings, some authorities having jurisdiction (AHJ) have 
required alternate occupancy classification to more restrictive, Group B, Division 2 treatment occupancy buildings.  
Some reasons include:  
• the ability of occupants to self-evacuate. 
• AHJ assesses levels of care as treatment occupancy or assesses that transitional or crisis intervention should 
presume a level of care as a treatment occupancy.  
• the design of the building capable of being a treatment occupancy. 
 
The definitions within the NBC(AE) for care and treatment occupancies are not necessarily interchangeable with 
defined or described terms for supportive living under the Continuing Care Act. Each regulation has its own 
context and objectives: for instance, supportive living residences regulated under continuing care regulations may 
be classified as a Group B, Division 3 care occupancy or a Group C residential occupancy. Accommodations 
where treatment is provided are regulated under other legislation and a Group B, Division 2 treatment occupancy 
classification would apply. 
 
An assessment conducted by a Case Manager (CM) or Health Care Professional (HCP) is required in the 
decision process for the proper placing of a patient/client/resident. The duty and authority for assessment is with 
the CM or HCP, not with the AHJ. 
 
It is not the position of an AHJ to provide an assessment or 
---Application---
The proposed construction of a building or part thereof in which the intent is to provide care services in the 
occupancy without treatment or to provide residential accommodations without required care services.  
 
Interpretation 
1. Where a building is intended and designated by the owner to provide care services falling within the scope of 
Group B, Division 3 care occupancy classification, and no treatment is intended to be provided, the Group B, 
Division 3 care occupancy classification would be applicable.  
2. Where a building is intended and designated by the owner to provide Group C, residential accommodations 
with no provision of required care services falling within the scope of Group B, Division 3 care occupancy 
classification, the Group C, residential occupancy classification would be applicable.   
 
 
 
This INTERPRETATION is applicable throughout the Province of Alberta. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
---Interpretation---
1. Where a building is intended and designated by the owner to provide care services falling within the scope of 
Group B, Division 3 care occupancy classification, and no treatment is intended to be provided, the Group B, 
Division 3 care occupancy classification would be applicable.  
2. Where a building is intended and designated by the owner to provide Group C, residential accommodations 
with no provision of required care services falling within the scope of Group B, Division 3 care occupancy 
classification, the Group C, residential occupancy classification would be applicable.   
 
 
 
This INTERPRETATION is applicable throughout the Province of Alberta. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 

===== standata-nbc2023-interpretation-23-bci-020 =====
 
Gas STANDATA | Alberta.ca  
Building STANDATA | Alberta.ca 
©2026 Government of Alberta | March 2026  |  Municipal Affairs 
  
STANDATA interpretation 25-GCI-003/23-BCI-020 
Gas/Building  
  
Mechanically vented appliances 
Date Issued:  March 2026 
Page 1 of 4 
Purpose 
To clarify what types of appliances are considered to meet the definitions of mechanically vented and direct -
vented in the National Building Code – 2023 Alberta Edition (NBC(AE)) and, subsequently, when make-up air 
would need to be provided within a dwelling unit to protect against the effects of depressurization . 
Discussion 
The NBC(AE) contains two defined terms for situations where make-up air would not be required to be supplied to 
a dwelling unit to offset the potential hazard from depressurization of the dwelling unit and the subsequent 
spillage of products of combustion from fuel-fired appliances. Under Sentence 9.32.3.8.(1) dwelling units that 
have fuel-fired space-heating or water-heating appliances do not require make-up air if they are mechanically 
vented or direct-vented appliances as defined in the NBC(AE). 
Safety codes officers and industry have asked for additional clarification regarding the types of fuel -fired 
appliances that would meet the definition of mechanically vented. Some safety codes officers have interpreted the 
requirements such that if there is any mixing of the air from the space where the appliance is located and 
products of combustion (regardless of the design of the appliance and any safety features which may be present), 
then the appliance is considered to be at risk for the spillage of products of combustion, in which case make-up 
air must be provided. 
The CSA B149.1 Natural gas and propane installation code makes reference to specific categories of fuel -fired 
appliances in the definition of “appliance”. All fuel-burning appliances in Canada are required to be classified to 
receive certification, including any modifications or accessories to the appliance that might change the 
classification category. 
Category I and II appliances often hav
---Application---
This interpretation applies to Part 9 buildings and the application of Article 9.32.3.8. to mitigate the effects of 
depressurization. 
Interpretation 
1. Category I and II appliances do not meet the definition of either mechanically vented or direct -vented. As 
such, any dwelling unit with a category I or II appliance installed within it must be protected from the effects of 
depressurization in accordance with the requirements of NBC(AE) Article 9.32.3.8.  
 
2. All categories III and IV appliances are considered to meet the definition of mechanically vented. Any dwelling 
unit with categories III or IV fuel-fired space-heating appliance or fuel-fired water-heating appliance does not 
need to be protected from the effects of depressurization in accordance with the requirements of NBC(AE) 
Article 9.32.3.8.  
 
3. Appliances that are installed such that combustion air and products of combustion are directly supplied from 
and discharged to the exterior via independent, totally enclosed passageways that are connected directly to 
the appliance are considered to meet the definition of direct-vented and does not need to be protected from 
the effects of depressurization in accordance with the requirements of NBC(AE) Article 9.32.3.8.  
 
This INTERPRETATION is applicable throughout the Province of Alberta.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Unless stated otherwise, all Code references in this STANDATA are to B149. 1 Natural gas and propane 
installation code, and Division B of the National Building Code – 2023 Alberta Edition 
Issued by the Provincial Gas and Building Administrators

---Interpretation---
1. Category I and II appliances do not meet the definition of either mechanically vented or direct -vented. As 
such, any dwelling unit with a category I or II appliance installed within it must be protected from the effects of 
depressurization in accordance with the requirements of NBC(AE) Article 9.32.3.8.  
 
2. All categories III and IV appliances are considered to meet the definition of mechanically vented. Any dwelling 
unit with categories III or IV fuel-fired space-heating appliance or fuel-fired water-heating appliance does not 
need to be protected from the effects of depressurization in accordance with the requirements of NBC(AE) 
Article 9.32.3.8.  
 
3. Appliances that are installed such that combustion air and products of combustion are directly supplied from 
and discharged to the exterior via independent, totally enclosed passageways that are connected directly to 
the appliance are considered to meet the definition of direct-vented and does not need to be protected from 
the effects of depressurization in accordance with the requirements of NBC(AE) Article 9.32.3.8.  
 
This INTERPRETATION is applicable throughout the Province of Alberta.

appropriately. This interpretation provides a process for labeling renovated Part 10 units.

===== standata-nbc2023-interpretation-23-bci-022 =====

STANDATA interpretation 23-BCI-022 
Building  
Energy efficiency exemptions for buildings 
Date Issued:  May 2025 
Page 1 of 2 
Purpose 
To assist in identifying certain types and uses of buildings, such as buildings constructed to shelter equipment 
which has been established to be exempt from Section 9.36., “Energy Efficiency,” of the National Building Code – 
2023 Alberta Edition (NBC(AE)) and the National Energy Code of Canada for Buildings 2020 (NECB).  
Discussion 
Section 9.36. and the NECB are the currently enforced energy efficiency requirements for buildings, developed by 
the National Research Council of Canada as part of the commitment to improve the energy efficiency of buildings 
and reduce greenhouse gas emissions. Section 9.36. and the NECB cover a wide range of building components 
and systems, including building envelope, electrical, and mechanical systems.  
Some exemptions are permitted in both Section 9.36. or the NECB regardless of the occupancy classification of 
the building. The application of an exemption is based on use, occupancy, and specific building types and is 
determined on a case-by-case basis. When requested, the designer must provide the authority having jurisdiction 
(AHJ) sufficient information including references to the energy code indicating why a building may be exempted.  
The type or intended use of some buildings may be such that compliance with Section 9.36. or NECB 
requirements would be impractical. 
Sentence 2.2.1.1.(2) of Division C of the NECB permits the AHJ to review such special cases and to permit 
exemptions. 
Sentence 9.36.1.3.(7) of the NBC(AE) provides for exemptions where the building is not required to be a 
conditioned space. 
 
 
Code References 
NECB 2020 Division A and NBC(AE) 1.4.1.2. 
1.4.1.2. Defined Terms 
Conditioned space means any space within a building, the temperature of which is controlled to limit 
variation in response to the exterior ambient temperature by the provision, either direct
---Application---
This interpretation applies to industrial occupancies not intended to be normally occupied by persons or sheltering 
equipment, and specific building types to which it would be impractical to comply with energy efficiency 
requirements.  
This interpretation is intended to provide guidance but may not be applicable to every possible scenario. For 
irregular or unique cases, designers shall contact the AHJ. 
Interpretation 
The following are exempt from mandatory compliance with Section 9.36. and the NECB:  
1. Industrial occupancy buildings not normally occupied by persons (no offices,  no washrooms, etc.). These 
buildings are intended for storage or to shelter equipment, and access is limited to workers who occasionally 
enter the building to perform specific short duration tasks (such as maintaining or servicing of equipment). 
Equipment could include, but is not limited to: compressors, pumps, heaters, boilers, turbines, air 
compressors, separators, treaters, dehydrators, chemical packages, refrigeration, deluge control systems, fire 
water pumps, electrical substations, power distribution, instrumentation packages, motor con trol centers 
(MCC), variable frequency drive (VFD) units, and pumping equipment.  
2. Tents and air-, cable-, and frame-supported membrane structures. 
 
This INTERPRETATION is applicable throughout the Province of Alberta.  
Unless stated otherwise, all Code references in this STANDATA are to Division B of the National Building Code –  
2023 Alberta Edition 
Issued by the Provincial Building Administrator

===== standata-nbc2023-interpretation-23-bci-023 =====
 
 
Building STANDATA | Alberta.ca  
©2026 Government of Alberta  |  January 2026  |  Municipal Affairs 
 
   
STANDATA interpretation 23-BCI-023 
Building  
“Building Envelope” definition related to attic or roof spaces 
Date Issued:  January 2026 
Page 1 of 2  
Purpose 
This interpretation clarifies the intended meaning of “building envelope” in the National Building Code –  2023 
Alberta Edition (NBC(AE)) as it relates to the expected performance of attic or roof spaces. 
Discussion 
There are different interpretations of the term “building envelope” being used throughout Alberta, and this poses 
design and enforcement difficulties, particularly as it relates to attic or roof spaces (see defined term in Code 
References section). 
The National Energy Code for Buildings 2020 (NECB) contains a definition for the term “building envelope,” which 
has been applied by some in the context of the NBC(AE). Although Sentence 1.4.1.1.(1) of Division A of the 
NBC(AE) allows terms to have meanings that have been assigned through other Codes or Regulations under the 
Safety Codes Act, the definition of “building envelope” under the NECB reflects a meaning specifically within the 
context of thermal transmission and does not include all aspects of building envelope performance. 
 
Every material, component and/or assembly that serves or adjoins an attic or roof space has a pivotal role in 
contributing to the overall performance of the building envelope. For example:  
• The roof assembly and exterior wall assemblies of an attic or roof space may not be thermally insulated; 
however, these assemblies serve the envelope purpose of preventing the ingress of precipitation (rain, snow, 
etc.) into the building. 
• The NBC(AE) requires an unconditioned attic or roof space to be ventilated to promote the transfer of moist air 
from this space to the exterior air outside the building. Limiting the risk of moisture  accumulation, condensation, 
or ice dams ultimately serves to protect the health of the building occupants and prevent the damage or 
deterioration of building elements.
---Application---
This interpretation applies to buildings of residential occupancy containing one or more dwelling units.  
Interpretation 
The term “building envelope,” as it relates to the expected performance of an attic or roof space, shall have the 
following meaning: 
1. Building envelope means the collection of materials, components and/or assemblies that : 
• separate an attic or roof space from the exterior air or the ground,  
• provide the ventilation of an unconditioned attic or roof space to permit the transfer of moisture from the space 
to the exterior air, or 
• separate conditioned space from an unconditioned attic or roof space.  
 
 
 
This INTERPRETATION is applicable throughout the Province of Alberta.  
 
 
 
 
[original signed] 
 
 
 
 
 
---Interpretation---
The term “building envelope,” as it relates to the expected performance of an attic or roof space, shall have the 
following meaning: 
1. Building envelope means the collection of materials, components and/or assemblies that : 
• separate an attic or roof space from the exterior air or the ground,  
• provide the ventilation of an unconditioned attic or roof space to permit the transfer of moisture from the space 
to the exterior air, or 
• separate conditioned space from an unconditioned attic or roof space.  
 
 
 
This INTERPRETATION is applicable throughout the Province of Alberta.  
 
 
 
 
[original signed]

STANDATA interpretation 23-BCI-024/23-FCI-008 
Building/Fire  
  
Variance process for industrial occupancy  
Date Issued:  August 2026 
Page 1 of 6 
Purpose 
To clarify the variance application, review, and enforcement process for authorities having jurisdiction (AHJs) and 
designers that may allow for a high-hazard industrial (F-1) occupancy to be classified as a medium-hazard 
industrial (F-2) occupancy, or for F-1 to be addressed as presenting a similar risk to an F-2. 
This also addresses the relevant Safety Codes Act (SCA) and Code requirements and is not a design or 
evaluation guide for the required engineering analysis. 
Discussion 
The National Building Code – 2023 Alberta Edition (NBC(AE)) and the National Fire Code – 2023 Alberta Edition 
(NFC(AE)) establish major occupancy classifications for various types of occupancies or building uses.   
In Alberta, designers have challenged the F-1 occupancy classification for certain industrial occupancies. An F-1 
occupancy currently does not allow an attached restaurant or beverage establishment within the same building. A 
variance provides a path by which a restaurant or beverage establishment may be included in the same building 
as the following occupancies, but not limited to a flour mill, distillery, or cereal mill. 
Designers have advocated that the current Codes are outdated or overly restrictive for certain industrial 
occupancy classifications, compared to building and fire codes in jurisdictions outside of Canada reflect ing current 
industry practices. Designers have asserted that it can be demonstrated their proposed design poses a hazard 
level equivalent to an F-2 occupancy. AHJs in Alberta have required designers to rely upon an assortment of 
interpretations and variances to allow for this change in classification.   
This patchwork of interpretations and variances has resulted in proposed re-cla
---Application---
This interpretation applies to a building or part thereof with an F-1 occupancy classification under the NBC(AE) or 
NFC(AE). 
 
Interpretation 
1. An AHJ may accept the occupancy classification of an F-1 occupancy as an equivalent risk to an F-2 
occupancy as part of a variance application, provided that the following conditions are met:  
 
• A n engineering analysis  demonstrates that no special hazard exists , or that the special hazard is 
appropriately addressed through hazard control systems , such that a safety performance level 
approximately equivalent to or greater than an F-2 occupancy is achieved. 
• The engineering analysis is  performed by a registered engineering professional  (as defined by the 
NBC(AE)) who is authorized and qualified to engage in such work.  
• The documentation submitted to support the variance is in conformance with Section 2.3. of Division C 
of the NBC(AE). 
• Applicable terms and conditions are provided in support of the variance application. 
 
2. To address ongoing Code compliance following occupancy, the owner must have the variance documentation 
available on the premises for review by the fire SCO, to confirm that the engineering analysis conditions under 
which the variance was issued have not been contravened. 
This INTERPRETATION is applicable throughout the Province of Alberta.  
 
Unless stated otherwise, all Code references in this STANDATA are to the National Building Code – 2023 Alberta 
Edition or National Fire Code – 2023 Alberta Edition 
Issued by the Provincial Building and Fire Administrators

occupancy as part of a variance application, provided that the following conditions are met:  
 
• A n engineering analysis  demonstrates that no special hazard exists , or that the special hazard is

STANDATA interpretation 23-BCI-025/24-ECI-009 
Building/Electrical
Service/utility room for energy storage systems 
Date Issued: October 2026 
Page 1 of 4 
Purpose 
To clarify that a building safety codes officer (SCO) reviews and inspects the design and construction of a room 
containing energy storage systems (ESS) where the room provisions are in the 2024 Canadian Electrical Code, 
Part 1 (CE Code Part 1). 
Discussion 
Electrical and building SCOs have raised concerns over which discipline administers the requirements in CE 
Code Part 1, Rule 64-1100 1) d), 2) and 11), pertaining to the construction of a room containing an ESS. 
The Safety Codes Act (SCA) along with its 21 regulations establishes a unifying framework for the administration 
of 11 safety disciplines, with each having their own safety codes and standards.  
An SCO administers the SCA, the regulations reference the applicable codes which fall within the scope of 
administering the SCA. 
Under Clause 6(1) of the Permit Regulation, a permit in the building discipline is required for the construction of a 
building, including the renovation of or addition to a building. As such, if construction in the building discipline is 
undertaken, a building permit is required. 
The National Building Code - 2023 Alberta Edition (NBC(AE)) references CAN/CSA-B72, “Installation Code 
for Lightning Protection Systems.”  A lightening protection system is an electrical system falling within the 
scope of the Electrical SCO for permit and inspection. 
Code References  
C22.1:24- 2024 Canadian Electrical Code, Part 1 
Section 0 — Object, scope, and definitions 
Definitions 
Service room — a room or space provided in a building to accommodate building service equipment 
and constructed in accordance with the National Building Code of Canada or applicable local legislation 
(see Appendix B, Note to Rule 26-010). 
STANDATA 23-BCI-025/24-ECI-009 Page 2 of 4 
alberta.ca/building-standata
---Application---
This interpretation applies to the review and inspection of a service/utility room for the purpose of complying with 
CE Code Rule 64-1100 1)d), 2) and 11) 
Interpretation 
1. A room is required by the CE Code to enclose an ESS; the construction of the room is to be reviewed and
inspected for compliance with the CE Code by a building SCO. 
2. In addition to an electrical permit being required for the electrical installation of an ESS, a building permit is
required for the construction of room containing an ESS. 
This INTERPRETATION is applicable throughout the Province of Alberta.

===== standata-nbc2023-interpretation-23-bci-026 =====
